What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Compare product ranges using a defined sell-through measure and a balanced buying recommendation.
  • Explain price-volume arithmetic and challenge a profit claim without dismissing the promotion goal.
  • Translate inventory states into an accurate location-specific availability message.
  • Acknowledge a missed delivery estimate, distinguish shipment from arrival, and explain a concrete follow-up.
  • Report a conversion change accurately and distinguish an observed association from a causal claim.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Merchandising and Assortment PlanningEqual revenue, different stock exposure+

Your goal

Compare product ranges using a defined sell-through measure and a balanced buying recommendation.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Equal revenue, different stock exposure

During the same four-week season, Range A sold 80 of its 100 opening units for $12,000. Range B sold 120 of its 200 opening units, also for $12,000. There were no receipts, returns, or stock adjustments. A sold at full price; B required markdowns. For this review, unit sell-through means units sold divided by opening units. Buyer Inez and planner Cal must discuss next season without assuming that equal revenue means equal performance. Product costs and customer-segment evidence are not yet available.

Inez: Buyer / Cal: Merchandise planner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Range A's sell-through under the stated definition?

Answer and explanation

D. 80%

Eighty units sold divided by one hundred opening units equals eighty percent.

2. How many units remain in Range B?

Answer and explanation

A. 80

Two hundred opening units minus one hundred twenty sold leaves eighty units.

3. What is not yet established?

Answer and explanation

C. Which range has higher gross profit

Product costs are missing, so equal revenue does not establish relative gross profit.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Sell-through definitions differ. This case uses units sold divided by opening units, with no other stock movements. A is 80%; B is 60%. Do not quietly substitute revenue or remaining units as the denominator.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
assortment
The selection of products offered to customers.review the assortment
range
A related group of products offered for sale.compare product ranges
stock-keeping unit (SKU)
An internal identifier for a specific stocked item or variant.rationalize the SKU count
sell-through
Units sold as a proportion of a defined stock base.calculate unit sell-through
opening stock
Inventory held at the start of a period.establish opening stock
residual stock
Inventory remaining after the measured selling period.reduce residual stock
markdown
A reduction from an earlier selling price.take a markdown
full-price sale
A sale at the regular undiscounted price.protect full-price sales
option count
The number of distinct product choices in a range.review the option count
assortment breadth
The variety of categories or product choices offered.broaden the assortment
assortment depth
The variety of options within a product category or line, distinct from units held per option.adjust assortment depth
size curve
The planned distribution of units across sizes.revise the size curve
colorway
A particular color version of a product.add a colorway
core line
A recurring product line central to the offer.replenish a core line
seasonal line
A line intended for a particular selling season.exit a seasonal line
range review
A structured assessment of the product selection.conduct a range review
buying budget
Funds allocated to purchasing merchandise.protect the buying budget
open-to-buy
A planned merchandise purchasing allowance after commitments.check open-to-buy
stock turn
A measure of how frequently inventory is sold or replaced.compare stock turn
weeks of cover
Stock expressed in weeks at a specified sales rate.assess weeks of cover
gross margin return on inventory
Gross profit relative to average inventory cost.evaluate inventory return
delist
Remove an item from the active product selection.propose a delisting
range rationalization
Simplifying the assortment using stated commercial criteria.support range rationalization
customer segment
A group of customers with relevant shared characteristics.serve a customer segment

Equal sales revenue does not establish equal gross profit or inventory return. Costs, timing, stock investment, and the role of each range matter. A recommendation can identify further analysis without pretending the final buying decision is settled.

C Notice the language

Challenge the measure, not the buyer

A range can matter for customer choice even when its stock turns slowly. Recognize that commercial purpose, then identify the missing evidence. A precise question about margin or remaining stock is more useful than calling a colleague's selection a failure.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the comparison
Both ranges generated $12,000 in the same four weeks.
Define the measure
Here, sell-through uses units sold divided by opening stock.
State the stronger rate
Range A sold through at eighty percent.
State the remaining exposure
Range B still has eighty units on hand.
Acknowledge the commercial role
B may serve a different customer segment.
Separate units and revenue
More units sold does not necessarily mean more revenue.
Identify the price effect
B reached the same revenue with markdowns.
Ask for the missing basis
Can we add product costs before comparing gross profit?
Avoid an automatic decision
This is not enough evidence to delist the whole range.
Narrow the next review
Let us examine B by size and colorway.
Protect a useful distinction
The rate is lower even though unit sales are higher.
Request comparable periods
Keep both ranges on the same four-week basis.
Limit the recommendation
I recommend a more targeted buy, subject to the missing evidence.
Clarify commitment
A proposal is not a released purchase order.
Preserve the customer question
We still need to understand which customers bought B.
Summarize the trade-off
The same revenue came with different stock and price exposure.
6 language and precision notes
At full price
Describes the transaction price, not automatically a higher profit.
Sold through at
Introduces a rate with an explicitly defined stock base.
Subject to
Makes the recommendation conditional on named evidence.
Even though
Connects a higher unit count with a lower sell-through rate.
By size and colorway
Narrows analysis to commercially useful product variants.
Not enough evidence
Challenges the conclusion without dismissing the data.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. A sold 80% of opening units; B sold 60%.

Each range needs its own opening-unit denominator; revenue does not replace that stock base.

2. Which claim needs product costs?

Answer and explanation

B. A earned more gross profit than B.

Gross profit subtracts product costs, which the briefing does not supply.

3. Which recommendation is appropriately limited?

Answer and explanation

C. Review B by variant and customer segment before changing the buy.

Variant and segment evidence supports a more specific decision than the aggregate result alone.

4. Which wording states a condition?

Answer and explanation

B. We can revise the buy once the cost and variant review is complete.

Once makes the proposed action depend on completing the specified review.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Equal revenue, different stock exposure

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Inez: Buyer / Cal: Merchandise planner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • assortment depth
  • delisting
  • gross margin return on inventory
  • customer segment
  • residual stock
  • sell-through
  • range review
  • buying budget
  • markdown
  • size curve
  1. Inez
    Both ranges brought in twelve thousand. Can we repeat the buy? B needed more promotional support, but I do not want to cut a range customers still use.
  2. Cal
    Before we repeat the buy, look at 1. A sold eighty of one hundred units; B sold one hundred twenty of two hundred. That gives eighty percent and sixty percent respectively.
  3. Inez
    B did sell forty more units. I do not want the team to lose sight of that demand when we review the range.
  4. Cal
    Agreed, but B also leaves more 2. We have eighty units remaining there, compared with twenty in A. Those units still need a commercial plan.
  5. Inez
    We reached the same revenue through different prices. A held its regular price, while B required reductions during the season.
  6. Cal
    Exactly. The 3 helped B move units, but we cannot judge the profit effect properly without product costs. The revenue line alone will not settle that question.
  7. Inez
    Should we remove B completely? That would release buying budget, but it might also leave a gap for a customer group we serve.
  8. Cal
    I would not recommend an immediate 4. First, separate B by size and colorway. An aggregate result can hide strong variants alongside weak ones.
  9. Inez
    Some sizes may be carrying the leftovers. We should see whether the original distribution matched what customers actually selected.
  10. Cal
    Then review the 5. That may support changing quantities within the range rather than removing every option. We need the variant-level data before deciding.
  11. Inez
    I also want to know whether B attracts customers who rarely buy A. Equal revenue does not tell us whether the ranges overlap.
  12. Cal
    Yes, include the 6 evidence. We should not describe B as unnecessary until we understand its role. That is a separate question from this period's lower rate.
  13. Inez
    Could we call for a smaller initial order, with more room to react later?
  14. Cal
    We can propose that within the 7, but supplier lead times and minimum quantities still need checking. Flexibility on paper is not always available in the supply arrangement.
  15. Inez
    Let us keep the recommendation conditional and show the remaining inventory separately.
  16. Cal
    I will add 8 once we have the required cost and average-stock information. I will not manufacture it from these sales totals.
  17. Inez
    Good. Keep the four-week dates visible so we do not compare a full season with a partial one.
  18. Cal
    The 9 will show common dates, both denominators, the price reductions, and the outstanding analysis. That makes the recommendation easier to challenge constructively.
  19. Inez
    Hold the repeat order, then. Bring me the size and color breakdown with costs before we decide which options to keep.
  20. Cal
    Correct. We can revisit 10 after that review, alongside quantities per option. Equal revenue is the starting observation, not the final buying decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. sell-through Turn 2. Sell-through compares sold units with the defined stock base, revealing a difference that equal revenue hides.
  2. residual stock Turn 4. Residual stock is the inventory left after sales; B retains eighty units while A retains twenty.
  3. markdown Turn 6. Markdown names the price reduction used to support B's sales, not the sales volume itself.
  4. delisting Turn 8. Delisting removes a product from the active range; the evidence does not yet justify removing all of B.
  5. size curve Turn 10. The size curve describes the unit distribution across sizes, which may explain uneven leftover stock.
  6. customer segment Turn 12. Customer segment identifies the group served by a range and helps assess its distinct commercial role.
  7. buying budget Turn 14. Buying budget names the money allocated to merchandise purchases, constraining the proposed initial order.
  8. gross margin return on inventory Turn 16. This measure relates gross profit to average inventory cost, requiring cost and investment information absent here.
  9. range review Turn 18. Range review is the structured assessment bringing together sales, stock, pricing, and the role of the selection.
  10. assortment depth Turn 20. Assortment depth concerns the variety of options within a line, such as sizes and colorways; units held per option are stock depth.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Inez: Both ranges brought in twelve thousand. Can we repeat the buy? B needed more promotional support, but I do not want to cut a range customers still use.
  2. Cal: Before we repeat the buy, look at sell-through. A sold eighty of one hundred units; B sold one hundred twenty of two hundred. That gives eighty percent and sixty percent respectively.
  3. Inez: B did sell forty more units. I do not want the team to lose sight of that demand when we review the range.
  4. Cal: Agreed, but B also leaves more residual stock. We have eighty units remaining there, compared with twenty in A. Those units still need a commercial plan.
  5. Inez: We reached the same revenue through different prices. A held its regular price, while B required reductions during the season.
  6. Cal: Exactly. The markdown helped B move units, but we cannot judge the profit effect properly without product costs. The revenue line alone will not settle that question.
  7. Inez: Should we remove B completely? That would release buying budget, but it might also leave a gap for a customer group we serve.
  8. Cal: I would not recommend an immediate delisting. First, separate B by size and colorway. An aggregate result can hide strong variants alongside weak ones.
  9. Inez: Some sizes may be carrying the leftovers. We should see whether the original distribution matched what customers actually selected.
  10. Cal: Then review the size curve. That may support changing quantities within the range rather than removing every option. We need the variant-level data before deciding.
  11. Inez: I also want to know whether B attracts customers who rarely buy A. Equal revenue does not tell us whether the ranges overlap.
  12. Cal: Yes, include the customer segment evidence. We should not describe B as unnecessary until we understand its role. That is a separate question from this period's lower rate.
  13. Inez: Could we call for a smaller initial order, with more room to react later?
  14. Cal: We can propose that within the buying budget, but supplier lead times and minimum quantities still need checking. Flexibility on paper is not always available in the supply arrangement.
  15. Inez: Let us keep the recommendation conditional and show the remaining inventory separately.
  16. Cal: I will add gross margin return on inventory once we have the required cost and average-stock information. I will not manufacture it from these sales totals.
  17. Inez: Good. Keep the four-week dates visible so we do not compare a full season with a partial one.
  18. Cal: The range review will show common dates, both denominators, the price reductions, and the outstanding analysis. That makes the recommendation easier to challenge constructively.
  19. Inez: Hold the repeat order, then. Bring me the size and color breakdown with costs before we decide which options to keep.
  20. Cal: Correct. We can revisit assortment depth after that review, alongside quantities per option. Equal revenue is the starting observation, not the final buying decision.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Similar revenue, different sell-through

Fictional assortment review: two ranges have similar revenue over the same season, but range B sells through more slowly and needs larger markdowns; costs are not fully assessed.

  1. Category manager: Both ranges generated similar sales revenue. Should we give them the same space next season?
  2. Merchandise planner: Range B sold through more slowly and needed larger markdowns, so revenue alone hides important differences.
  3. Category manager: Then we should compare sell-through, markdowns, and gross margin using the same season and scope.
  4. Merchandise planner: Yes. We also need comparable cost information before describing either assortment as more profitable.
  5. Category manager: Could B still serve a useful customer need even if its stock moves more slowly?
  6. Merchandise planner: Possibly, but that role needs evidence. We shouldn't infer it merely to defend the current allocation.
  7. Category manager: I'll request the customer and SKU-level information alongside the matched financial comparison.
  8. Merchandise planner: Good. Keep the tradeoff explicit: similar revenue, but different inventory movement and discounting requirements.
  9. Category manager: I'll avoid calling the ranges equivalent and ask the team which criteria matter for next season.
  10. Merchandise planner: That gives us a grounded assortment discussion rather than allocating space from one headline number.
Fewer SKUs or broader choice?

Separate fictional assortment case: range A offers six SKUs and B twelve, with the same planned opening stock value. Customer-demand evidence and replenishment costs are incomplete.

  1. Buying manager: Both plans use the same opening stock value, but A has six SKUs and B twelve.
  2. Assortment analyst: Then the choice involves depth versus breadth, not simply a larger or smaller initial budget.
  3. Buying manager: Could we assume twelve SKUs will double the number of customers the range attracts?
  4. Assortment analyst: No. The broader choice doesn't establish demand, and our customer evidence is incomplete.
  5. Buying manager: What matching criteria should we use before deciding how many products to launch?
  6. Assortment analyst: Compare expected demand, stock depth, replenishment needs, sell-through, and margin on the same planning basis.
  7. Buying manager: A may support deeper stock per item, while B offers more variety under the fixed opening value.
  8. Assortment analyst: Yes, as a planning tradeoff. We still need the item-level quantities before claiming exact stock depth.
  9. Buying manager: I'll ask the team for that detail and avoid promising fewer stockouts from either plan.
  10. Assortment analyst: Good. The recommendation should explain both the verified differences and the assumptions still awaiting evidence.
A high margin percentage can hide slow movement

Separate fictional range comparison: A has a higher stated gross-margin percentage but slower sell-through than B; identical-period total margin contribution has not been calculated.

  1. Retail buyer: Range A has the higher gross-margin percentage. Is it automatically the stronger assortment choice?
  2. Merchandising analyst: Not automatically. It also sells through more slowly, and total margin contribution hasn't been compared.
  3. Retail buyer: So percentage margin and the amount of margin earned across the period answer different questions.
  4. Merchandising analyst: Exactly. We need matching periods, quantities, markdowns, and costs to compare overall contribution fairly.
  5. Retail buyer: Could the slower movement also matter because stock occupies space that other SKUs could use?
  6. Merchandising analyst: Yes, that's a relevant planning question, but we should assess it rather than invent a cost.
  7. Retail buyer: I'll present A's percentage advantage alongside B's faster movement, without claiming either settles the decision.
  8. Merchandising analyst: Good. Ask for the period-level contribution and inventory data before recommending the next allocation.
  9. Retail buyer: We'll use the same criteria for both ranges and keep missing information visible in the meeting.
  10. Merchandising analyst: That makes the tradeoff understandable without confusing one attractive ratio with the whole commercial outcome.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Equal revenue, different stock exposure
  1. Read turns 1-10; contrast units sold, sell-through, and remaining stock.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep the next buy conditional on the cost and variant review.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Compare a second pair of ranges

Range C sells 45 of 60 opening units. Range D sells 60 of 120 opening units. There are no other stock movements. Use the same unit sell-through definition.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Buyer: "C has a sell-through rate of ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 75%

Forty-five sold units divided by sixty opening units equals seventy-five percent.

2. Planner: "D has a sell-through rate of ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 50%

Sixty sold units divided by one hundred twenty opening units equals fifty percent.

3. Buyer: "C has ___ units remaining."

Answer and explanation

A. 15

Sixty opening units less forty-five sold leaves fifteen remaining units.

4. Planner: "D has ___ units remaining."

Answer and explanation

A. 60

One hundred twenty opening units less sixty sold leaves sixty remaining units.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Lesson 01: Merchandising and Assortment Planning
Original fictional case: During the same four-week season, Range A sold 80 of its 100 opening units for $12,000. Range B sold 120 of its 200 opening units, also for $12,000. There were no receipts, returns, or stock adjustments. A sold at full price; B required markdowns. For this review, unit sell-through means units sold divided by opening units. Buyer Inez and planner Cal must discuss next season without assuming that equal revenue means equal performance. Product costs and customer-segment evidence are not yet available.
Language workshop: Equal revenue, different stock exposure
Communication goal: Compare product ranges using a defined sell-through measure and a balanced buying recommendation.
Language guidance: Sell-through definitions differ. This case uses units sold divided by opening units, with no other stock movements. A is 80%; B is 60%. Do not quietly substitute revenue or remaining units as the denominator.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both ranges generated $12,000 in the same four weeks. / Here, sell-through uses units sold divided by opening stock. / Range A sold through at eighty percent. / Range B still has eighty units on hand. / B may serve a different customer segment. / More units sold does not necessarily mean more revenue. / B reached the same revenue with markdowns. / Can we add product costs before comparing gross profit? / This is not enough evidence to delist the whole range. / Let us examine B by size and colorway. / The rate is lower even though unit sales are higher. / Keep both ranges on the same four-week basis. / I recommend a more targeted buy, subject to the missing evidence. / A proposal is not a released purchase order. / We still need to understand which customers bought B. / The same revenue came with different stock and price exposure.
Vocabulary: assortment: The selection of products offered to customers. / range: A related group of products offered for sale. / stock-keeping unit (SKU): An internal identifier for a specific stocked item or variant. / sell-through: Units sold as a proportion of a defined stock base. / opening stock: Inventory held at the start of a period. / residual stock: Inventory remaining after the measured selling period. / markdown: A reduction from an earlier selling price. / full-price sale: A sale at the regular undiscounted price. / option count: The number of distinct product choices in a range. / assortment breadth: The variety of categories or product choices offered. / assortment depth: The variety of options within a product category or line, distinct from units held per option. / size curve: The planned distribution of units across sizes. / colorway: A particular color version of a product. / core line: A recurring product line central to the offer. / seasonal line: A line intended for a particular selling season. / range review: A structured assessment of the product selection. / buying budget: Funds allocated to purchasing merchandise. / open-to-buy: A planned merchandise purchasing allowance after commitments. / stock turn: A measure of how frequently inventory is sold or replaced. / weeks of cover: Stock expressed in weeks at a specified sales rate. / gross margin return on inventory: Gross profit relative to average inventory cost. / delist: Remove an item from the active product selection. / range rationalization: Simplifying the assortment using stated commercial criteria. / customer segment: A group of customers with relevant shared characteristics.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; contrast units sold, sell-through, and remaining stock. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep the next buy conditional on the cost and variant review. Complete the transfer and check each range against its own opening stock.
Partner: Inez: Buyer / Cal: Merchandise planner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Range C sells 45 of 60 opening units. Range D sells 60 of 120 opening units. There are no other stock movements. Use the same unit sell-through definition.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Inez: Good. Keep the four-week dates visible so we do not compare a full season with a partial one.
Cal: The range review will show common dates, both denominators, the price reductions, and the outstanding analysis. That makes the recommendation easier to challenge constructively.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Professional audience: retail managers, e-commerce teams, merchandising staff, store operations leaders, fulfillment teams, customer service managers, marketplace sellers, and growth operators
Scope: All companies, customers, products, figures, and local procedures are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal, accounting, or operational advice. Actual consumer rights, return obligations, marketplace terms, and shipping requirements depend on the transaction and jurisdiction. Store policies do not override applicable rights. Software terminology varies by platform.
Lesson 01: Merchandising and Assortment Planning
Original fictional case: During the same four-week season, Range A sold 80 of its 100 opening units for $12,000. Range B sold 120 of its 200 opening units, also for $12,000. There were no receipts, returns, or stock adjustments. A sold at full price; B required markdowns. For this review, unit sell-through means units sold divided by opening units. Buyer Inez and planner Cal must discuss next season without assuming that equal revenue means equal performance. Product costs and customer-segment evidence are not yet available.
Language workshop: Equal revenue, different stock exposure
Communication goal: Compare product ranges using a defined sell-through measure and a balanced buying recommendation.
Language guidance: Sell-through definitions differ. This case uses units sold divided by opening units, with no other stock movements. A is 80%; B is 60%. Do not quietly substitute revenue or remaining units as the denominator.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Both ranges generated $12,000 in the same four weeks. / Here, sell-through uses units sold divided by opening stock. / Range A sold through at eighty percent. / Range B still has eighty units on hand. / B may serve a different customer segment. / More units sold does not necessarily mean more revenue. / B reached the same revenue with markdowns. / Can we add product costs before comparing gross profit? / This is not enough evidence to delist the whole range. / Let us examine B by size and colorway. / The rate is lower even though unit sales are higher. / Keep both ranges on the same four-week basis. / I recommend a more targeted buy, subject to the missing evidence. / A proposal is not a released purchase order. / We still need to understand which customers bought B. / The same revenue came with different stock and price exposure.
Vocabulary: assortment: The selection of products offered to customers. / range: A related group of products offered for sale. / stock-keeping unit (SKU): An internal identifier for a specific stocked item or variant. / sell-through: Units sold as a proportion of a defined stock base. / opening stock: Inventory held at the start of a period. / residual stock: Inventory remaining after the measured selling period. / markdown: A reduction from an earlier selling price. / full-price sale: A sale at the regular undiscounted price. / option count: The number of distinct product choices in a range. / assortment breadth: The variety of categories or product choices offered. / assortment depth: The variety of options within a product category or line, distinct from units held per option. / size curve: The planned distribution of units across sizes. / colorway: A particular color version of a product. / core line: A recurring product line central to the offer. / seasonal line: A line intended for a particular selling season. / range review: A structured assessment of the product selection. / buying budget: Funds allocated to purchasing merchandise. / open-to-buy: A planned merchandise purchasing allowance after commitments. / stock turn: A measure of how frequently inventory is sold or replaced. / weeks of cover: Stock expressed in weeks at a specified sales rate. / gross margin return on inventory: Gross profit relative to average inventory cost. / delist: Remove an item from the active product selection. / range rationalization: Simplifying the assortment using stated commercial criteria. / customer segment: A group of customers with relevant shared characteristics.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; contrast units sold, sell-through, and remaining stock. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep the next buy conditional on the cost and variant review. Complete the transfer and check each range against its own opening stock.
Partner: Inez: Buyer / Cal: Merchandise planner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Range C sells 45 of 60 opening units. Range D sells 60 of 120 opening units. There are no other stock movements. Use the same unit sell-through definition.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Inez: Good. Keep the four-week dates visible so we do not compare a full season with a partial one.
Cal: The range review will show common dates, both denominators, the price reductions, and the outstanding analysis. That makes the recommendation easier to challenge constructively.
Inez: Hold the repeat order, then. Bring me the size and color breakdown with costs before we decide which options to keep.
Cal: Correct. We can revisit assortment depth after that review, alongside quantities per option. Equal revenue is the starting observation, not the final buying decision.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Pricing, Promotions, and MarginA busy promotion with a smaller gross profit+

Your goal

Explain price-volume arithmetic and challenge a profit claim without dismissing the promotion goal.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A busy promotion with a smaller gross profit

In the baseline week, a product sold 100 units at $50 each. During the promotion it sold 120 units at $42.50 each, a 15% price reduction. Product cost remained $30 per unit. These are complete comparable weekly sales with no returns. Taxes, shipping income, operating expenses, and other promotional costs are excluded. Trading manager Hugo says the 20% unit increase proves that profit improved. Finance partner Nia must correct that claim and separate the measured result from other possible campaign objectives.

Hugo: Trading manager / Nia: Finance partner

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What was promotion revenue?

Answer and explanation

A. $5,100

One hundred twenty units multiplied by forty-two dollars fifty equals five thousand one hundred dollars.

2. What was promotion gross profit on the stated basis?

Answer and explanation

C. $1,500

Revenue of $5,100 less product costs of $3,600 leaves $1,500 gross profit.

3. Which cost is excluded?

Answer and explanation

D. Operating expenses

The brief includes unit product cost but explicitly excludes operating expenses.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Units rose 20%, while price fell 15%. Revenue changes multiplicatively: 1.20 times 0.85 equals 1.02, a 2% increase. Subtracting the two percentages would incorrectly give 5%.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
selling price
The amount charged per unit before specified adjustments.set the selling price
unit volume
The number of units sold during a period.increase unit volume
sales revenue
Income from the measured sales before the stated costs.calculate sales revenue
cost of goods sold
Product costs attributable to the units sold.deduct cost of goods sold
gross profit
Sales revenue less cost of goods sold.protect gross profit
gross margin percentage
Gross profit divided by sales revenue, expressed as a percentage.calculate gross margin percentage
price reduction
A decrease in the selling price.apply a price reduction
promotional uplift
An increase against a defined nonpromotional comparison.measure promotional uplift
baseline
The reference level used for comparison.establish a baseline
incremental revenue
Additional revenue relative to the defined comparison.estimate incremental revenue
unit gross profit
Selling price less the stated product cost per unit.compare unit gross profit
contribution margin
Revenue less the variable costs included in its definition.define contribution margin
price elasticity
The responsiveness of quantity demanded to price changes.estimate price elasticity
cannibalization
Sales gained by one offer at another offer's expense.assess cannibalization
halo effect
A related benefit beyond the promoted product itself.test for a halo effect
basket value
The monetary value of a customer's purchase basket.increase basket value
average order value
Revenue divided by the relevant order count.monitor average order value
discount depth
The size of a price reduction against its reference.compare discount depth
redemption rate
The proportion using an offer among a defined eligible base.calculate redemption rate
promotion mechanic
The structure by which an offer operates.explain the promotion mechanic
funding allowance
A contribution toward promotion costs under agreed terms.verify a funding allowance
price override
An authorized change from the system's listed selling price.approve a price override
break-even volume
Units required to meet a specified cost or profit threshold.calculate break-even volume
margin dilution
A reduction in the relevant profit margin.quantify margin dilution

Baseline gross profit is $2,000; promotion gross profit is $1,500, down 25%. This is not net profit. Gross margin percentage falls from 40% to approximately 29.4% under the stated cost exclusions.

C Notice the language

Keep the success criterion explicit

A promotional team may emphasize traffic or new customers while finance emphasizes margin. Name the objective being tested. Correcting the arithmetic does not require calling the entire campaign a failure; other benefits need their own evidence.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge volume
The promotion sold twenty percent more units.
Correct the inference
That does not by itself establish higher profit.
State the new price
The promotional price was $42.50.
Keep the cost basis
Product cost stayed at thirty dollars per unit.
Calculate revenue
One hundred twenty times $42.50 gives $5,100.
Separate the measures
Revenue rose two percent; gross profit fell twenty-five percent.
Show the unit effect
Unit gross profit fell from twenty dollars to $12.50.
Name the exclusion
This calculation excludes operating and promotional expenses.
Define the margin
Gross margin percentage uses revenue as its denominator.
Avoid a false shortcut
The price and volume changes must be multiplied.
Recognize another objective
New-customer acquisition would need a separate measure.
Ask about funding
Has any supplier allowance actually been agreed?
Limit the conclusion
These figures do not establish net profitability.
Explain the threshold
At $12.50 per unit, 160 units would match the prior gross profit.
Protect the baseline
Use the same product and comparable periods.
Close the review
Let us report the volume gain and the margin cost together.
6 language and precision notes
By itself
Restricts what one measure can prove.
From ... to ...
Makes the original and new values explicit.
Excludes
Names costs or revenues outside the calculation.
Approximately
Marks a rounded percentage, not an exact value.
Would match
States a conditional threshold, not an achieved result.
Actually agreed
Distinguishes confirmed funding from hoped-for support.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement correctly combines the price and volume effects?

Answer and explanation

C. Revenue rose 2%.

The new quantity factor 1.20 multiplied by price factor 0.85 gives revenue factor 1.02.

2. What happened to gross profit?

Answer and explanation

D. It fell from $2,000 to $1,500.

Baseline gross profit is 100 times $20; promotion gross profit is 120 times $12.50.

3. How many promotional units would match baseline gross profit?

Answer and explanation

D. 160

The $2,000 baseline divided by $12.50 unit gross profit requires 160 units.

4. Which claim goes beyond the supplied evidence?

Answer and explanation

D. The campaign acquired profitable new customers.

No customer-acquisition or customer-profitability evidence is supplied by the weekly sales figures.

E Conversations

A busy promotion with a smaller gross profit

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hugo: Trading manager / Nia: Finance partner

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • baseline
  • unit volume
  • gross profit
  • cost of goods sold
  • break-even volume
  • margin dilution
  • Sales revenue
  • unit gross profit
  • gross margin percentage
  • funding allowance
  1. Hugo
    Units were up twenty percent. I have called that a profit improvement in the trading note. Can you check the wording before I send it?
  2. Nia
    The 1 improved, but the price fell. Let us calculate the money result before choosing that wording. We sold one hundred twenty units at forty-two dollars fifty.
  3. Hugo
    That gives five thousand one hundred dollars, compared with five thousand in the normal week.
  4. Nia
    Correct. 2 increased two percent. We multiply the quantity and price factors, rather than subtracting fifteen percent from twenty percent and calling the difference five.
  5. Hugo
    Revenue is still higher. Why would that not support the profit claim?
  6. Nia
    Because 3 also increased with the extra units. At thirty dollars per unit, it was three thousand dollars before and three thousand six hundred during the promotion.
  7. Hugo
    So we subtract those product costs from the revenue in each week.
  8. Nia
    Yes. That gives 4 of two thousand dollars before and fifteen hundred during the promotion. On that measure, the result fell twenty-five percent despite the higher revenue.
  9. Hugo
    We should also explain why each sale contributed less before the other costs.
  10. Nia
    The 5 fell from twenty dollars to twelve dollars fifty. We would need one hundred sixty promotional units to match the previous two thousand dollars.
  11. Hugo
    We sold only one hundred twenty, so we did not reach that threshold.
  12. Nia
    Right. This 6 is defined as matching the prior gross profit, not covering every business expense. The definition needs to stay next to the number.
  13. Hugo
    Could traffic or new customers still make the promotion worthwhile?
  14. Nia
    Possibly, but those goals need evidence beyond this 7 comparison. We have no new-customer or repeat-purchase data here. A possible benefit should not be presented as a measured result.
  15. Hugo
    The buyer mentioned possible supplier funding. Do we have an agreed amount, or should I leave it out of this result?
  16. Nia
    Only if the 8 is confirmed and accounted for on the correct basis. At present, these figures contain no agreed supplier contribution. Keep that possibility separate.
  17. Hugo
    What percentage should the margin line show?
  18. Nia
    The 9 is approximately twenty-nine point four percent, down from forty. That is a revenue-based ratio, not the twenty-five-percent change in gross profit dollars.
  19. Hugo
    I will report the volume gain and the lower gross profit, then list customer acquisition as an unanswered question.
  20. Nia
    Good. Describe the 10 clearly without declaring every campaign objective a failure. We can assess the wider decision when the additional evidence is available.

Answers and explanations
  1. unit volume Turn 2. Unit volume counts units sold; the twenty-percent increase does not alone establish revenue or profit improvement.
  2. Sales revenue Turn 4. Sales revenue is price multiplied by units here; it rose from five thousand to five thousand one hundred dollars.
  3. cost of goods sold Turn 6. Cost of goods sold includes thirty dollars for each sold unit, rising as unit volume increases.
  4. gross profit Turn 8. Gross profit subtracts product costs from revenue; the resulting amounts are two thousand and fifteen hundred dollars.
  5. unit gross profit Turn 10. Unit gross profit subtracts the thirty-dollar product cost from each selling price, producing twenty dollars and twelve fifty.
  6. break-even volume Turn 12. Break-even volume here is explicitly the quantity needed to match prior gross profit, not company-wide net-profit break-even.
  7. baseline Turn 14. Baseline identifies the reference week against which this promotion is compared, not proof of wider customer benefits.
  8. funding allowance Turn 16. Funding allowance is supplier support under agreed terms; an unconfirmed possibility must not be booked as an established benefit.
  9. gross margin percentage Turn 18. Gross margin percentage divides gross profit by revenue; fifteen hundred over fifty-one hundred is approximately twenty-nine point four percent.
  10. margin dilution Turn 20. Margin dilution names the reduction in the profit margin, allowing a balanced account alongside the higher sales volume.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hugo: Units were up twenty percent. I have called that a profit improvement in the trading note. Can you check the wording before I send it?
  2. Nia: The unit volume improved, but the price fell. Let us calculate the money result before choosing that wording. We sold one hundred twenty units at forty-two dollars fifty.
  3. Hugo: That gives five thousand one hundred dollars, compared with five thousand in the normal week.
  4. Nia: Correct. Sales revenue increased two percent. We multiply the quantity and price factors, rather than subtracting fifteen percent from twenty percent and calling the difference five.
  5. Hugo: Revenue is still higher. Why would that not support the profit claim?
  6. Nia: Because cost of goods sold also increased with the extra units. At thirty dollars per unit, it was three thousand dollars before and three thousand six hundred during the promotion.
  7. Hugo: So we subtract those product costs from the revenue in each week.
  8. Nia: Yes. That gives gross profit of two thousand dollars before and fifteen hundred during the promotion. On that measure, the result fell twenty-five percent despite the higher revenue.
  9. Hugo: We should also explain why each sale contributed less before the other costs.
  10. Nia: The unit gross profit fell from twenty dollars to twelve dollars fifty. We would need one hundred sixty promotional units to match the previous two thousand dollars.
  11. Hugo: We sold only one hundred twenty, so we did not reach that threshold.
  12. Nia: Right. This break-even volume is defined as matching the prior gross profit, not covering every business expense. The definition needs to stay next to the number.
  13. Hugo: Could traffic or new customers still make the promotion worthwhile?
  14. Nia: Possibly, but those goals need evidence beyond this baseline comparison. We have no new-customer or repeat-purchase data here. A possible benefit should not be presented as a measured result.
  15. Hugo: The buyer mentioned possible supplier funding. Do we have an agreed amount, or should I leave it out of this result?
  16. Nia: Only if the funding allowance is confirmed and accounted for on the correct basis. At present, these figures contain no agreed supplier contribution. Keep that possibility separate.
  17. Hugo: What percentage should the margin line show?
  18. Nia: The gross margin percentage is approximately twenty-nine point four percent, down from forty. That is a revenue-based ratio, not the twenty-five-percent change in gross profit dollars.
  19. Hugo: I will report the volume gain and the lower gross profit, then list customer acquisition as an unanswered question.
  20. Nia: Good. Describe the margin dilution clearly without declaring every campaign objective a failure. We can assess the wider decision when the additional evidence is available.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

More units do not establish higher profit

Fictional promotion review: units sold rise 20% and selling price falls 15%, compared with the same base period; costs and sales-mix effects are not supplied.

  1. Promotions manager: Units rose twenty percent during the promotion. Can we say profit definitely increased?
  2. Commercial analyst: The selling price fell fifteen percent, and we haven't assessed costs or the sales mix.
  3. Promotions manager: If the base was one hundred units at one hundred each, what would that show?
  4. Commercial analyst: Under that simplified example, one hundred twenty at eighty-five gives revenue of ten thousand two hundred.
  5. Promotions manager: So revenue rises only two percent in that example, not twenty percent, before considering costs.
  6. Commercial analyst: Correct. Revenue isn't profit, and the example doesn't include other factors in the actual promotion.
  7. Promotions manager: Could markdown costs or cannibalization of other sales change the overall result for the category?
  8. Commercial analyst: Yes, those require assessment. We shouldn't attribute every extra unit to genuinely additional demand.
  9. Promotions manager: I'll report the observed unit and price changes and request a matched gross-margin analysis.
  10. Commercial analyst: Good. Keep the arithmetic illustration separate from any unverified conclusion about total promotional profit.
Featured sales rise while neighboring items fall

Separate fictional promotion case: a featured SKU's sales rise while neighboring SKUs decline; total category results and causal effects remain unassessed.

  1. Category promotions lead: The featured SKU sold more, so the promotion must have expanded demand for the whole category.
  2. Retail analyst: Neighboring SKUs sold less, and we haven't calculated the category total or established the cause.
  3. Category promotions lead: Could that pattern indicate cannibalization, with shoppers switching from one product to another?
  4. Retail analyst: It may, but the pattern alone doesn't establish switching or explain every change.
  5. Category promotions lead: Then I'll describe the observed product changes and keep the category-wide conclusion open.
  6. Retail analyst: Good. We need comparable periods, product scope, and margin information before judging the promotion.
  7. Category promotions lead: Would excluding the declining SKUs make the analysis clearer because they weren't featured?
  8. Retail analyst: It could hide an important effect. Define the scope transparently rather than selecting only favorable results.
  9. Category promotions lead: I'll request the whole-category view and distinguish the possible explanation from the confirmed sales figures.
  10. Retail analyst: That lets the team discuss cannibalization without treating a plausible story as an established outcome.
A markdown clears stock but may reduce contribution

Separate fictional clearance review: a markdown reduces remaining stock from 60 units to 10; associated margin and costs have not been calculated.

  1. Store trading manager: The markdown reduced remaining stock from sixty units to ten. Can we call it a profit success?
  2. Margin analyst: It achieved stock clearance, but we haven't calculated the margin contribution or associated costs.
  3. Store trading manager: Then selling fifty units establishes movement, not whether the financial result met our target.
  4. Margin analyst: Exactly. The clearance objective and the profit question need separate evidence in the review.
  5. Store trading manager: Could I say the markdown prevented future losses because those units are no longer on hand?
  6. Margin analyst: That future comparison isn't established. We don't know what would have happened without the markdown.
  7. Store trading manager: I'll keep the confirmed stock reduction and ask for the realized selling prices and relevant costs.
  8. Margin analyst: Good. Include the same reporting period and any returns when the team assesses gross margin.
  9. Store trading manager: Our update will say clearance improved, with financial contribution still under review rather than guaranteed.
  10. Margin analyst: That recognizes the observed outcome without adding an unsupported claim about profit or avoided losses.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A busy promotion with a smaller gross profit
  1. Read turns 1-10; say both revenue and gross profit before assessing the promotion.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the profit-matching threshold from actual sales.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Calculate another promotion result

A product sells 50 units at $80 in the baseline period and 60 units at $70 in the promotion. Product cost is $50 per unit. Exclude all other costs and returns.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "Baseline revenue was ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 4,000

Fifty units multiplied by eighty dollars yields four thousand dollars of revenue.

2. Analyst: "Promotion revenue was ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 4,200

Sixty units multiplied by seventy dollars yields four thousand two hundred dollars.

3. Manager: "Baseline gross profit was ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

B. 1,500

Fifty units times the thirty-dollar unit gross profit gives fifteen hundred dollars.

4. Analyst: "Promotion gross profit was ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. 1,200

Sixty units times the twenty-dollar unit gross profit gives twelve hundred dollars.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.

5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Pricing, Promotions, and Margin
Original fictional case: In the baseline week, a product sold 100 units at $50 each. During the promotion it sold 120 units at $42.50 each, a 15% price reduction. Product cost remained $30 per unit. These are complete comparable weekly sales with no returns. Taxes, shipping income, operating expenses, and other promotional costs are excluded. Trading manager Hugo says the 20% unit increase proves that profit improved. Finance partner Nia must correct that claim and separate the measured result from other possible campaign objectives.
Language workshop: A busy promotion with a smaller gross profit
Communication goal: Explain price-volume arithmetic and challenge a profit claim without dismissing the promotion goal.
Language guidance: Units rose 20%, while price fell 15%. Revenue changes multiplicatively: 1.20 times 0.85 equals 1.02, a 2% increase. Subtracting the two percentages would incorrectly give 5%.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The promotion sold twenty percent more units. / That does not by itself establish higher profit. / The promotional price was $42.50. / Product cost stayed at thirty dollars per unit. / One hundred twenty times $42.50 gives $5,100. / Revenue rose two percent; gross profit fell twenty-five percent. / Unit gross profit fell from twenty dollars to $12.50. / This calculation excludes operating and promotional expenses. / Gross margin percentage uses revenue as its denominator. / The price and volume changes must be multiplied. / New-customer acquisition would need a separate measure. / Has any supplier allowance actually been agreed? / These figures do not establish net profitability. / At $12.50 per unit, 160 units would match the prior gross profit. / Use the same product and comparable periods. / Let us report the volume gain and the margin cost together.
Vocabulary: selling price: The amount charged per unit before specified adjustments. / unit volume: The number of units sold during a period. / sales revenue: Income from the measured sales before the stated costs. / cost of goods sold: Product costs attributable to the units sold. / gross profit: Sales revenue less cost of goods sold. / gross margin percentage: Gross profit divided by sales revenue, expressed as a percentage. / price reduction: A decrease in the selling price. / promotional uplift: An increase against a defined nonpromotional comparison. / baseline: The reference level used for comparison. / incremental revenue: Additional revenue relative to the defined comparison. / unit gross profit: Selling price less the stated product cost per unit. / contribution margin: Revenue less the variable costs included in its definition. / price elasticity: The responsiveness of quantity demanded to price changes. / cannibalization: Sales gained by one offer at another offer's expense. / halo effect: A related benefit beyond the promoted product itself. / basket value: The monetary value of a customer's purchase basket. / average order value: Revenue divided by the relevant order count. / discount depth: The size of a price reduction against its reference. / redemption rate: The proportion using an offer among a defined eligible base. / promotion mechanic: The structure by which an offer operates. / funding allowance: A contribution toward promotion costs under agreed terms. / price override: An authorized change from the system's listed selling price. / break-even volume: Units required to meet a specified cost or profit threshold. / margin dilution: A reduction in the relevant profit margin.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; say both revenue and gross profit before assessing the promotion. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the profit-matching threshold from actual sales. Complete the transfer using price times units, then subtract the stated product costs.
Partner: Hugo: Trading manager / Nia: Finance partner
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A product sells 50 units at $80 in the baseline period and 60 units at $70 in the promotion. Product cost is $50 per unit. Exclude all other costs and returns.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Hugo: What percentage should the margin line show?
Nia: The gross margin percentage is approximately twenty-nine point four percent, down from forty. That is a revenue-based ratio, not the twenty-five-percent change in gross profit dollars.
Hugo: I will report the volume gain and the lower gross profit, then list customer acquisition as an unanswered question.
Nia: Good. Describe the margin dilution clearly without declaring every campaign objective a failure. We can assess the wider decision when the additional evidence is available.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
