Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A redesign and an outage overlap

Fictional digital-retail analysis: checkout conversion fell after a redesign, while payment outages occurred in the same period; causal contribution is unassessed.

  1. Digital product manager: Checkout conversion fell after the redesign. Can we conclude the new interface caused the decline?
  2. Ecommerce analyst: Payment outages occurred in the same period, so the observed timing doesn't isolate the redesign's effect.
  3. Digital product manager: Then the funnel data shows a decline, but the explanation remains uncertain in this comparison.
  4. Ecommerce analyst: Exactly. We should review payment failures, comparable traffic, and the measurement setup before attributing cause.
  5. Digital product manager: Would calling it an A/B test make the result more convincing to the leadership team?
  6. Ecommerce analyst: Only if a genuine controlled comparison was conducted. A before-and-after observation isn't automatically an A/B test.
  7. Digital product manager: I'll avoid that label and ask for the outage-period breakdown and consistent conversion-rate definitions.
  8. Ecommerce analyst: Good. Also keep cart abandonment distinct from confirmed payment failures; those events may need separate analysis.
  9. Digital product manager: The update will report the decline and overlapping outage, without announcing a proven design failure.
  10. Ecommerce analyst: That allows a focused investigation rather than a confident explanation unsupported by the current evidence.
A tracking change can alter the denominator

Separate fictional analytics case: a tracking revision changes which sessions are counted, and reported conversion rate rises; purchase totals have not yet been reconciled.

  1. Online trading manager: Conversion rate rose after the tracking update. Did the website become better at selling?
  2. Digital analytics lead: The counted-session definition changed, so the rate may not be comparable with the earlier report.
  3. Online trading manager: Then a smaller denominator could affect the percentage even if purchases didn't increase.
  4. Digital analytics lead: Yes, but we still need the reconciled counts before explaining the actual movement here.
  5. Online trading manager: Could we publish the higher rate as an improvement and add the definition change later?
  6. Digital analytics lead: That would invite a misleading comparison. State the measurement change alongside the reported figure.
  7. Online trading manager: I'll request matching session definitions and verified purchase totals before attributing improvement to user experience.
  8. Digital analytics lead: Good. Keep the tracking issue separate from a claim about the effectiveness of the checkout funnel.
  9. Online trading manager: The current update will say reported conversion rose under a changed measurement definition.
  10. Digital analytics lead: And that a like-for-like performance conclusion remains pending until the underlying counts are aligned.
Five usability sessions do not establish a sales uplift

Separate fictional UX review: four of five test participants finish a prototype checkout; no live sales experiment has been conducted.

  1. UX researcher: Four of five participants completed the prototype checkout. Can the slide claim higher live conversion?
  2. Experimentation manager: The sessions show performance in that small test, not a measured change in live conversion rate.
  3. UX researcher: Then we should describe the observed task completion and avoid turning it into an ecommerce revenue result.
  4. Experimentation manager: Exactly. The prototype setting, participant sample, and task instructions differ from actual shopping traffic.
  5. UX researcher: Would an A/B test be one possible way to evaluate the live change, subject to planning?
  6. Experimentation manager: Yes, but we haven't designed or run one here, so don't imply experimental evidence exists.
  7. UX researcher: I'll preserve the usability finding and identify the untested claim about cart abandonment and purchases.
  8. Experimentation manager: Good. Also avoid saying the one incomplete session proves every shopper will face that difficulty.
  9. UX researcher: I'll report the specific observed issue and recommend review without extending the sample beyond its scope.
  10. Experimentation manager: That makes the evidence useful for design work while leaving live commercial impact unresolved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A checkout decline with two competing explanations
  1. Read turns 1-10; say the decline in percentage points and as a relative percentage.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate purchasing sessions from purchase events.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Explain a second conversion comparison

A fixed report counts 300 purchasing sessions out of 600 eligible sessions before a change and 240 out of 600 afterward. One session counts at most once.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Lead: "The original rate was ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 50%

Three hundred purchasing sessions divided by six hundred eligible sessions equals fifty percent.

2. Analyst: "The later rate was ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 40%

Two hundred forty purchasing sessions divided by six hundred eligible sessions equals forty percent.

3. Lead: "The absolute decline was ___."

Answer and explanation

B. ten percentage points

Fifty percent minus forty percent is an absolute ten-percentage-point difference.

4. Analyst: "The relative decline was ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 20%

Ten percentage points divided by the original fifty-percent rate equals a twenty-percent relative decline.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Retail and E-Commerce English
Professional audience: retail managers, e-commerce teams, merchandising staff, store operations leaders, fulfillment teams, customer service managers, marketplace sellers, and growth operators
Scope: All companies, customers, products, figures, and local procedures are fictional. This book teaches professional English, not legal, accounting, or operational advice. Actual consumer rights, return obligations, marketplace terms, and shipping requirements depend on the transaction and jurisdiction. Store policies do not override applicable rights. Lesson 05: Conversion, UX, and Digital Analytics
Original fictional case: Before a checkout redesign, 400 of 1,000 eligible checkout sessions completed a purchase. Afterward, 320 of 1,000 did so. For this report, checkout conversion is completed purchasing sessions divided by eligible checkout sessions; one session counts at most once. A payment-provider outage also occurred in the later period. Event collection and traffic mix have not yet been verified as comparable. Product lead Mara and analyst Dev must explain the decline without claiming that the redesign alone caused it.
Language workshop: A checkout decline with two competing explanations
Communication goal: Report a conversion change accurately and distinguish an observed association from a causal claim.
Language guidance: The conversion rate fell from 40% to 32%: eight percentage points, or a 20% relative decline from the original rate. These are two descriptions of the same change, not interchangeable units.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Checkout conversion fell from forty to thirty-two percent. / That is a decline of eight percentage points. / Relative to the original rate, the decline is twenty percent. / We count a session once if it completes a purchase. / Both periods contain one thousand eligible checkout sessions. / A payment-provider outage overlapped the later period. / This comparison does not isolate the redesign's effect. / Has event collection remained consistent across the release? / More purchase events do not necessarily mean more purchasing sessions. / Compare traffic and device mix before interpreting the aggregate. / Separate affected and unaffected time intervals. / That breakdown would be informative, not automatically causal. / Uncertain attribution does not make the decline unimportant. / Validate the data, then compare the relevant segments. / A rollback decision should use the full risk and performance evidence. / We have a measured decline and an unresolved explanation.
Vocabulary: checkout conversion: Completed purchasing sessions divided by the specified checkout-session base. / conversion funnel: The ordered steps toward a defined customer action. / checkout abandonment: Failure to complete a purchase after entering checkout. / eligible session: A session meeting the stated inclusion criteria. / purchase event: A recorded event representing a purchase action. / event instrumentation: The implementation of event collection in a digital product. / duplicate event: An event recorded more than once for the same action. / deduplication: Identifying and handling repeated records of the same event. / denominator: The base quantity used in a rate calculation. / percentage point: One unit of difference between percentage rates. / relative change: A change expressed as a proportion of its initial value. / traffic mix: The composition of visitors by relevant source or characteristic. / device segment: A group of users or sessions using a specified device type. / payment failure: An unsuccessful payment attempt under the relevant process. / payment gateway: A service connecting a checkout to payment processing. / provider outage: A disruption to a third-party service. / confounder: A factor that complicates a causal interpretation. / causal attribution: Assigning an observed outcome to its cause. / controlled experiment: A comparison designed to isolate an intervention's effect. / random assignment: Allocation by chance to comparison groups. / cohort: A group sharing a defined characteristic or starting event. / user experience (UX): A person's experience of interacting with a product or service. / friction: An obstacle making a customer action harder. / rollback: Returning a change to an earlier version.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; say the decline in percentage points and as a relative percentage. Swap roles for turns 11-20; separate purchasing sessions from purchase events. Complete the transfer and check the denominator before explaining the change.
Partner: Mara: Digital product lead / Dev: Commerce analyst
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fixed report counts 300 purchasing sessions out of 600 eligible sessions before a change and 240 out of 600 afterward. One session counts at most once.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mara: We still need to make a release decision. Which checks can we finish first, and what would remain uncertain afterward?
Dev: Agreed. Evaluate a rollback alongside the actual risks and diagnostics. My caution concerns the explanation, not whether the decline deserves urgent attention.
Mara: I will report the two rates, the outage, and the checks we are doing before claiming a cause.
Dev: That keeps causal attribution honest. We have a real reported decline, several testable questions, and a clear next analysis. That is more useful than blaming the release from one chart.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Marketplace and Vendor ManagementThe supplier with more defects has the lower rate+

Your goal

Challenge a vendor scorecard by specifying the unit, denominator, period, and comparison limits.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

The supplier with more defects has the lower rate

A July scorecard shows Vendor K with ten defective units and Vendor M with five. The heading says K has the higher defect rate. Receiving records show 800 units received from K and 200 from M in July. Count each defective unit once, even if it has several faults; all counts refer to these July receipts. Product mix and inspection intensity have not been confirmed as comparable. Vendor manager Rosa and supplier representative Malik must correct the rate comparison before discussing causes or commercial deductions.

Rosa: Vendor manager / Malik: Supplier representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Vendor K's defective-unit rate?

Answer and explanation

D. 1.25%

Ten defective units divided by eight hundred received units equals one point two five percent.

2. What is Vendor M's defective-unit rate?

Answer and explanation

C. 2.5%

Five defective units divided by two hundred received units equals two point five percent.

3. What remains unverified?

Answer and explanation

D. Comparable product mix and inspection intensity

The brief explicitly leaves product mix and inspection intensity unconfirmed as comparable.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A count is not a rate. K has more defective units but a lower defective-unit rate: 10/800 = 1.25%, compared with M at 5/200 = 2.5%. Each defective unit is counted only once.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
vendor scorecard
A report comparing supplier performance against defined measures.correct a vendor scorecard
defective-unit rate
Defective units divided by the specified unit population.calculate the defective-unit rate
defect count
The number of individual faults identified.distinguish the defect count
received units
Units recorded as received within the defined scope.confirm received units
inspection intensity
The extent or frequency of examination.compare inspection intensity
product mix
The composition of products within a measured group.control for product mix
reporting period
The time interval covered by a measure.specify the reporting period
purchase order
A buyer's formal order specifying the relevant purchase terms.reconcile the purchase order
vendor allowance
A supplier-funded amount under agreed commercial terms.verify a vendor allowance
chargeback
A commercial deduction or charge asserted under applicable terms.dispute a vendor chargeback
supplier corrective action
An action addressing an established supplier-related problem.request supplier corrective action
root cause
The underlying reason for a problem, supported by investigation.establish the root cause
acceptance criterion
A requirement used to determine whether goods meet expectations.confirm the acceptance criterion
inspection record
Evidence of what was examined and found.retrieve the inspection record
sample size
The number of units examined in a sample.state the sample size
lot
A defined group of goods identified for traceability.trace the affected lot
return to vendor
Sending goods back to the supplying party.authorize a return to vendor
marketplace listing
A product offer displayed on a marketplace.update the marketplace listing
seller of record
The party identified as the seller for the transaction.identify the seller of record
catalog attribute
A structured product detail such as size or material.correct a catalog attribute
content compliance
Conformity with applicable product-content requirements.review content compliance
service-level agreement
Terms defining expected service performance and responsibilities.review the service-level agreement
dispute evidence
Records supporting a challenge to a claim or deduction.submit dispute evidence
remediation plan
A defined set of actions to address a problem.agree a remediation plan

A lower observed rate does not prove better inherent quality when inspection and product mix differ. The scorecard does not itself establish root cause, supplier liability, or the right to a commercial deduction.

C Notice the language

Make the disputed number reproducible

Supplier discussions can become adversarial when a scorecard is treated as unquestionable. Ask for the counted unit and denominator, then do the calculation together. Correcting a ranking does not excuse the underlying defects or establish which party caused them.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask for the base
What received-unit denominator is behind this rate?
State the period
Both figures refer to July receipts.
Clarify the counted object
Count each defective unit once, not every fault separately.
Correct the label
Ten is a count, not a percentage.
Show the calculation
Ten divided by eight hundred gives 1.25%.
Compare accurately
K has the larger count but the lower observed rate.
Keep defects visible
Correcting the ranking does not remove the ten defective units.
Check comparability
Were product mix and inspection intensity comparable?
Request traceable evidence
Please link the findings to the relevant lots and inspection records.
Avoid an unsupported cause
These numbers do not establish how the damage occurred.
Separate commercial authority
A scorecard result does not automatically authorize a deduction.
Check the terms
We need the applicable agreement before deciding any chargeback.
Keep the challenge constructive
I am challenging the rate label, not dismissing the reported defects.
Agree a correction
Let us republish the rates with their denominators.
Define the follow-up
Review the defect evidence and the comparison basis separately.
Close the dispute precisely
The arithmetic is corrected; the cause and commercial outcome remain open.
6 language and precision notes
Observed rate
Restricts the comparison to recorded data, not inherent supplier quality.
Count each ... once
Defines the unit and prevents double-counting multiple faults.
Behind this rate
Requests the denominator supporting the displayed percentage.
Not dismissing
Preserves the substantive concern while challenging its description.
Automatically
Flags an unsupported leap from a metric to an entitlement.
Remain open
Keeps unresolved questions distinct from the corrected arithmetic.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which ranking is arithmetically correct?

Answer and explanation

D. K has a lower defective-unit rate than M.

K's 1.25% rate is below M's 2.5% rate on the stated received-unit bases.

2. If one unit has three faults, how is it counted here?

Answer and explanation

C. As one defective unit

The case explicitly counts each defective unit once regardless of its number of faults.

3. Which claim still needs investigation?

Answer and explanation

A. K has better inherent quality under comparable conditions.

Product mix and inspection intensity may differ, so inherent comparative quality is not established.

4. Which action is not authorized merely by the scorecard?

Answer and explanation

B. Deducting a chargeback from the supplier's invoice

Commercial deductions require their applicable terms and basis, not just a performance label.

The supplier with more defects has the lower rate

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Vendor manager / Malik: Supplier representative

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  1. Rosa

  2. Malik
    Could we check the 1 behind those figures? I am not disputing the ten reported units, but the heading compares rates while the table only shows counts.
  3. Rosa
    The receiving file has eight hundred units from K and two hundred from M.
  4. Malik
    Then our 2 is one point two five percent, while M's is two point five percent. The larger defect count does not make our rate higher.
  5. Rosa
    You are right about the calculation. We should correct that before it goes into the supplier review.
  6. Malik
    Please also state the 3. I understand these counts all refer to July receipts. A rate could still be misleading if the defects came from a different receipt period.
  7. Rosa
    They all relate to July. Some units had several faults; the inspection team counted each affected unit once.
  8. Malik
    Good. That distinguishes defective units from 4. We should preserve that rule in the scorecard so next month's calculation does not silently change.
  9. Rosa
    Does the lower rate mean K has better quality overall?
  10. Malik
    It is a lower observed rate, but we need to compare 5 and inspection intensity before making that broader claim. I would not use the corrected arithmetic to dismiss the ten units.
  11. Rosa
    The inspection details should tell us what failed and how the goods were examined.
  12. Malik
    Please share the relevant 6 through the agreed channel. We can review the lot references and acceptance criteria rather than argue over an unexplained total.
  13. Rosa
    Operations suspects the packaging caused the damage, but the report has not established that.
  14. Malik
    Then packaging is a hypothesis, not a confirmed 7. We should examine the evidence and handling history before assigning responsibility. A useful correction depends on identifying the actual problem.
  15. Rosa
    Commercial wants to deduct a fee from the next invoice. Which inspection records and contract terms do we need to review with you first?
  16. Malik
    Any 8 needs the applicable terms and evidence. Correcting the rate does not decide that question, and the original heading does not automatically establish a right to deduct.
  17. Rosa
    I will separate the metric correction from the quality investigation and the commercial discussion.
  18. Malik
    That works. We can agree a 9 when the findings support the actions. Let us not promise a packaging change merely to close the meeting.
  19. Rosa
    I will republish both denominators, the July period, and the corrected percentages. The ten affected units will remain visible.
  20. Malik
    Thank you. The revised 10 will then support a fair discussion. We can acknowledge the quality concern while keeping the arithmetic, causal investigation, and contract issues distinct.

Answers and explanations
  1. received units Turn 2. Received units supply the stated rate denominator; ten and five alone are counts rather than rates.
  2. defective-unit rate Turn 4. Defective-unit rate divides defective units by received units here, yielding 1.25 percent for K and 2.5 percent for M.
  3. reporting period Turn 6. Reporting period fixes both numerator and denominator to July receipts so the comparison does not mix intervals.
  4. defect count Turn 8. Defect count counts individual faults; several faults on one unit must not become several defective units in this metric.
  5. product mix Turn 10. Product mix describes the goods included; different products can limit a claim about inherent comparative supplier quality.
  6. inspection record Turn 12. Inspection record supplies traceable findings and examination details needed to investigate the reported defective units.
  7. root cause Turn 14. Root cause is the supported underlying reason; the conversation supplies only an unconfirmed packaging hypothesis.
  8. chargeback Turn 16. Chargeback is a commercial deduction or charge whose basis must follow the applicable agreement, not the scorecard alone.
  9. remediation plan Turn 18. Remediation plan sets out actions addressing the problem once the evidence supports what needs to change.
  10. vendor scorecard Turn 20. Vendor scorecard should present defined measures accurately while leaving unresolved cause and commercial questions visible.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Malik: Could we check the received units behind those figures? I am not disputing the ten reported units, but the heading compares rates while the table only shows counts.
  3. Rosa: The receiving file has eight hundred units from K and two hundred from M.

  7. Rosa: They all relate to July. Some units had several faults; the inspection team counted each affected unit once.
  8. Malik: Good. That distinguishes defective units from defect count. We should preserve that rule in the scorecard so next month's calculation does not silently change.
  9. Rosa: Does the lower rate mean K has better quality overall?
  10. Malik: It is a lower observed rate, but we need to compare product mix and inspection intensity before making that broader claim. I would not use the corrected arithmetic to dismiss the ten units.
  11. Rosa: The inspection details should tell us what failed and how the goods were examined.
  12. Malik: Please share the relevant inspection record through the agreed channel. We can review the lot references and acceptance criteria rather than argue over an unexplained total.
  13. Rosa: Operations suspects the packaging caused the damage, but the report has not established that.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Define the defect-rate denominator

Fictional vendor review: a scorecard calls a defect rate high but omits the denominator and reporting period; no contractual breach is established.

  1. Marketplace vendor manager: Could you provide the defect count, denominator, and reporting period behind this vendor scorecard?
  2. Supplier account lead: The summary only says high defect rate. Do you need the underlying figures before our review meeting?
  3. Marketplace vendor manager: Yes. Without the basis, we can't interpret the rate or compare it with the agreed measure.
  4. Supplier account lead: Should I assume the denominator is orders because that is what our team usually counts?
  5. Marketplace vendor manager: Please confirm it from the source. It could be defined differently in this particular report.
  6. Supplier account lead: I'll request the calculation details and distinguish orders from units if the source does so.
  7. Marketplace vendor manager: Good. Also provide the relevant service-level agreement reference if the scorecard compares against an agreed target.
  8. Supplier account lead: Can I describe the vendor as breaching the SLA before those definitions are checked?
  9. Marketplace vendor manager: No breach is established here. We need a consistent, evidence-backed comparison and the applicable terms.
  10. Supplier account lead: I'll obtain the specific figures and confirm availability rather than defend or concede an unsupported claim.
Request comparable dispatch evidence

Separate fictional marketplace case: a vendor reports orders dispatched, while the retailer's scorecard uses carrier acceptance; the reporting period is the same.

  1. Vendor performance analyst: Could you clarify what dispatched means in your report and provide the matching carrier-acceptance evidence?
  2. Supplier operations coordinator: Our dispatched count records warehouse release, not the carrier's first acceptance scan.
  3. Vendor performance analyst: Thank you. That explains a definition difference, but we still need the comparable evidence for the scorecard.
  4. Supplier operations coordinator: Should we rename warehouse release as carrier acceptance so the reports use the same label?
  5. Vendor performance analyst: No. Changing a label wouldn't make the underlying events equivalent and would hide the discrepancy.
  6. Supplier operations coordinator: I'll request the acceptance records for the same reporting period and flag any missing scans.
  7. Vendor performance analyst: Good. Explain the gap without assuming every missing scan proves a late handover.
  8. Supplier operations coordinator: I'll confirm what records can be retrieved and ask for an achievable response point.
  9. Vendor performance analyst: That will support the SLA review while keeping any performance or breach conclusion pending.
  10. Supplier operations coordinator: I'll supply matching evidence, definitions, and limitations rather than a cosmetically aligned but inaccurate count.
A corrective proposal needs a named response

Separate fictional vendor case: a marketplace requested a plan for repeated packaging defects; the vendor replied we will improve without actions or ownership.

  1. Marketplace quality manager: Could you specify the proposed packaging actions and responsible contact behind your reply that you will improve?
  2. Vendor service manager: We intended to reassure you, but the reply didn't identify a concrete plan or owner.
  3. Marketplace quality manager: Please provide those details so we can review the response against the documented defect concerns.
  4. Vendor service manager: Do you need a guaranteed zero defect rate by next week as part of the plan?
  5. Marketplace quality manager: No. Don't promise an unsupported outcome. We need realistic actions, evidence, and an achievable update point.
  6. Vendor service manager: I'll consult the packaging lead and confirm what they can commit to after reviewing the cases.
  7. Marketplace quality manager: Good. Keep the proposal separate from a claim that the underlying issue is already resolved.
  8. Vendor service manager: Should I include the scorecard period and affected product references so the plan's scope is clear?
  9. Marketplace quality manager: Yes, using the approved information-sharing process and the actual cases rather than unrelated examples.
  10. Vendor service manager: I'll return a specific proposal with ownership and timing, while leaving acceptance and effectiveness for review.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The supplier with more defects has the lower rate
  1. Read turns 1-10; challenge the rate using each vendor's received-unit denominator.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish a corrected metric from a supported chargeback.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Correct a second supplier ranking

For the same month, Vendor P has six defective units among 600 received; Vendor Q has four among 200. Count each defective unit once.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "P's defective-unit rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 1%

Six divided by six hundred equals one percent on the specified received-unit basis.

2. Supplier: "Q's defective-unit rate is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 2%

Four divided by two hundred equals two percent on the specified received-unit basis.

3. Manager: "The larger defective-unit count belongs to ___."

Answer and explanation

C. P

P has six defective units compared with Q's four, so its count is larger.

4. Supplier: "The higher defective-unit rate belongs to ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Q

Q's two-percent rate exceeds P's one-percent rate despite Q's smaller defective-unit count.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Marketplace and Vendor Management
Original fictional case: A July scorecard shows Vendor K with ten defective units and Vendor M with five. The heading says K has the higher defect rate. Receiving records show 800 units received from K and 200 from M in July. Count each defective unit once, even if it has several faults; all counts refer to these July receipts. Product mix and inspection intensity have not been confirmed as comparable. Vendor manager Rosa and supplier representative Malik must correct the rate comparison before discussing causes or commercial deductions.
Language workshop: The supplier with more defects has the lower rate
Communication goal: Challenge a vendor scorecard by specifying the unit, denominator, period, and comparison limits.
Language guidance: A count is not a rate. K has more defective units but a lower defective-unit rate: 10/800 = 1.25%, compared with M at 5/200 = 2.5%. Each defective unit is counted only once.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What received-unit denominator is behind this rate? / Both figures refer to July receipts. / Count each defective unit once, not every fault separately. / Ten is a count, not a percentage. / Ten divided by eight hundred gives 1.25%. / K has the larger count but the lower observed rate. / Correcting the ranking does not remove the ten defective units. / Were product mix and inspection intensity comparable? / Please link the findings to the relevant lots and inspection records. / These numbers do not establish how the damage occurred. / A scorecard result does not automatically authorize a deduction. / We need the applicable agreement before deciding any chargeback. / I am challenging the rate label, not dismissing the reported defects. / Let us republish the rates with their denominators. / Review the defect evidence and the comparison basis separately. / The arithmetic is corrected; the cause and commercial outcome remain open.
Vocabulary: vendor scorecard: A report comparing supplier performance against defined measures. / defective-unit rate: Defective units divided by the specified unit population. / defect count: The number of individual faults identified. / received units: Units recorded as received within the defined scope. / inspection intensity: The extent or frequency of examination. / product mix: The composition of products within a measured group. / reporting period: The time interval covered by a measure. / purchase order: A buyer's formal order specifying the relevant purchase terms. / vendor allowance: A supplier-funded amount under agreed commercial terms. / chargeback: A commercial deduction or charge asserted under applicable terms. / supplier corrective action: An action addressing an established supplier-related problem. / root cause: The underlying reason for a problem, supported by investigation. / acceptance criterion: A requirement used to determine whether goods meet expectations. / inspection record: Evidence of what was examined and found. / sample size: The number of units examined in a sample. / lot: A defined group of goods identified for traceability. / return to vendor: Sending goods back to the supplying party. / marketplace listing: A product offer displayed on a marketplace. / seller of record: The party identified as the seller for the transaction. / catalog attribute: A structured product detail such as size or material. / content compliance: Conformity with applicable product-content requirements. / service-level agreement: Terms defining expected service performance and responsibilities. / dispute evidence: Records supporting a challenge to a claim or deduction. / remediation plan: A defined set of actions to address a problem.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; challenge the rate using each vendor's received-unit denominator. Swap roles for turns 11-20; distinguish a corrected metric from a supported chargeback. Complete the transfer and count each defective unit only once.
Partner: Rosa: Vendor manager / Malik: Supplier representative
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: For the same month, Vendor P has six defective units among 600 received; Vendor Q has four among 200. Count each defective unit once.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: I will separate the metric correction from the quality investigation and the commercial discussion.
Malik: That works. We can agree a remediation plan when the findings support the actions. Let us not promise a packaging change merely to close the meeting.
Rosa: I will republish both denominators, the July period, and the corrected percentages. The ten affected units will remain visible.
Malik: Thank you. The revised vendor scorecard will then support a fair discussion. We can acknowledge the quality concern while keeping the arithmetic, causal investigation, and contract issues distinct.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Customer Service and EscalationsA return request beyond the goodwill window+

Your goal

Acknowledge a customer request, explain a bounded exception review, and distinguish policy from applicable rights.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A return request beyond the goodwill window

Customer Owen wants to return an unworn $80 jacket because he changed his mind. His request is on day 35; the retailer states a 30-day change-of-mind return window for this purchase. No fault or misdescription is alleged. Agent Elena cannot approve a goodwill exception but can submit a review to the store manager. No review outcome is supplied. Before treating the policy as decisive, the team must check any applicable customer rights and transaction facts. A fault complaint would require a different assessment, not an automatic rejection under the goodwill window.

Owen: Customer / Elena: Service representative

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why does Owen want to return the jacket?

Answer and explanation

B. He changed his mind.

The briefing specifies a change of mind and supplies no fault or misdescription allegation.

2. What can Elena authorize herself?

Answer and explanation

C. Submission of a manager review, not approval of the exception

Elena can submit the request but does not hold the stated exception-approval authority.

3. How should the 30-day policy be treated?

Answer and explanation

D. As a stated policy that does not override applicable rights

The policy informs the request, but actual customer rights and transaction facts still require checking.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

This is a change-of-mind request on day 35, not an established fault claim. Record the actual reason accurately. The 30-day policy is a fictional retailer rule for this purchase, not a universal statement of consumer law.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
change-of-mind return
A return requested because the customer no longer wants the item.handle a change-of-mind return
return window
The period specified for a return under particular terms.check the return window
goodwill exception
A discretionary departure from policy where permitted.request a goodwill exception
exception authority
The defined power to approve a policy departure.confirm exception authority
proof of purchase
Evidence identifying a purchase and its relevant details.verify proof of purchase
purchase channel
The route through which the customer bought the item.identify the purchase channel
item condition
The physical state of a product.assess item condition
original payment method
The payment route used for the original purchase.verify the original payment method
store credit
Value available for a future purchase from the retailer.explain store credit
exchange
Replacing a purchased item with another under relevant terms.authorize an exchange
refund
Return of an amount paid.confirm a refund
manager review
Assessment by the person assigned the relevant decision authority.submit a manager review
fault allegation
A customer's statement that a product has a defect.record a fault allegation
misdescription
A mismatch between an item and its stated description.investigate misdescription
applicable rights
Customer protections relevant to the actual transaction.check applicable rights
statutory remedy
A remedy arising under the relevant law.distinguish a statutory remedy
policy discretion
Permitted choice within an organization's policy authority.explain policy discretion
escalation route
The defined path to a higher or specialist review.offer an escalation route
complaint record
A documented account of a customer's concern and its handling.maintain a complaint record
case owner
The person responsible for coordinating the case.identify the case owner
review outcome
The result of an assessment.communicate the review outcome
reason code
A structured label describing the basis for a transaction or request.select the correct reason code
resolution option
A possible way to address the customer's concern.verify resolution options
service handoff
Transfer of relevant context and responsibility to another team member.complete a service handoff

Submitting a review does not approve a refund, exchange, or store credit. Applicable rights must be checked separately; a retailer cannot replace a required remedy with discretionary goodwill merely by changing the label.

C Notice the language

Empathy is not an approval formula

An apologetic tone can accidentally sound like a refund commitment. Acknowledge the inconvenience, then state exactly what can happen next. Avoid describing a customer as difficult merely because the requested remedy is outside your authority.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the request
You would like to return the unworn jacket because you changed your mind.
Identify the timing
This request is on day thirty-five.
State the policy carefully
The stated change-of-mind window for this purchase is thirty days.
Keep rights separate
We also need to check the rights applicable to the actual transaction.
Explain your authority
I can submit the request, but I cannot approve an exception myself.
Offer the real next step
I can refer this to the store manager for review.
Avoid a guaranteed outcome
Submission does not guarantee a refund or exchange.
Request relevant evidence
Please provide the purchase details through our approved process.
Respect the customer's choice
Would you like me to submit that review?
Record the correct reason
I will record this as a change-of-mind request.
Distinguish a new allegation
If you are reporting a fault, I need to record and assess that separately.
Avoid an invented substitute
Store credit has not been approved either.
Reduce repetition
I will include the details you have already supplied.
Name the decision-maker
The manager holds the stated exception authority.
Keep the handoff useful
The record will show the timing, reason, item condition, and requested remedy.
Close honestly
I will communicate the actual review outcome without promising it in advance.
6 language and precision notes
For this purchase
Limits a policy statement to its actual transaction context.
I can ... but I cannot
Separates a permitted action from an unheld authority.
Would you like
Seeks the customer's choice about the offered review.
Has not been approved
Prevents an alternative remedy from being implied as available.
If you are reporting
Distinguishes a new factual allegation requiring a different assessment.
Without promising
Preserves a clear process commitment while leaving the decision open.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sentence stays within Elena's authority?

Answer and explanation

B. I can submit a manager review, but I cannot guarantee its outcome.

Elena can submit the request but cannot approve or promise the review result.

2. Which record best matches the stated request?

Answer and explanation

C. Change of mind; unworn jacket; day 35; $80 purchase

The correct record preserves the actual reason, condition, timing, and amount without inventing an outcome.

3. Which response to a newly alleged fault is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

B. Record the allegation and assess the applicable rights and process separately.

A fault allegation raises a different assessment and cannot be decided merely from this change-of-mind policy.

4. Which phrase asks permission without implying approval?

Answer and explanation

C. Would you like me to submit the review?

The question offers a specific submission action while leaving the review decision unmade.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

A return request beyond the goodwill window

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Owen: Customer / Elena: Service representative

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • store credit
  • return window
  • exception authority
  • manager review
  • fault allegation
  • proof of purchase
  • change-of-mind return
  • service handoff
  • complaint record
  • goodwill exception
  1. Owen
    I paid eighty dollars for this jacket and have not worn it. I chose the wrong style. I am five days outside the return window; is a review possible?
  2. Elena
    You are asking for a 1 on day thirty-five. I understand you would prefer not to keep an unworn item. Let me explain what I can do without promising a decision I cannot make.
  3. Owen
    The receipt says thirty days. Is there any way to ask someone to look at it?
  4. Elena
    Yes. The stated 2 is thirty days for this purchase. We also need to check the actual transaction and applicable rights rather than treat that policy as a universal legal answer.
  5. Owen
    I am not saying there is anything wrong with it. I simply chose the wrong style for myself.
  6. Elena
    I will record that accurately. I can request a 3, but I cannot approve one myself. The store manager can assess the request under the relevant process.
  7. Owen
    If you send it to the manager, does that mean I will get the money back?
  8. Elena
    No. A 4 is a request for a decision, not the decision itself. I do not want you to hear a refund promise when I am offering a review.
  9. Owen
    Would store credit be easier? I would be willing to buy something else here.
  10. Elena
    I can include that preference, but 5 has not been approved either. I should not present it as an automatic substitute simply because the original request is outside the stated window.
  11. Owen
    All right. What information does the manager need from me?
  12. Elena
    We need the relevant 6, the item condition, and your requested remedy through the approved process. I will include what you have already told me so you do not have to start again.
  13. Owen
    I have the receipt and the jacket with its tags. Please submit the request.
  14. Elena
    I will. The manager has the 7 described in our process. I will keep your reason and preferred outcomes clear rather than suggest the jacket is faulty to make the request sound stronger.
  15. Owen
    Suppose I later notice a fault. Would the thirty-day window automatically rule that out too?
  16. Elena
    A 8 would need to be recorded and assessed separately under the applicable rights and facts. I cannot decide that hypothetical claim from the change-of-mind policy alone.
  17. Owen
    That is clear. For now, it is just the style, and I would like the review.
  18. Elena
    The 9 will show the eighty-dollar jacket, unworn condition, day thirty-five, and your preference. It will not say a refund or exchange has already been agreed.
  19. Owen
    Please include the receipt details in the handoff. I would rather not start again when the manager responds.
  20. Elena
    I will preserve that in the 10. The next response should communicate the actual assessment and explain the available next step, not make you reconstruct this conversation.

Answers and explanations
  1. change-of-mind return Turn 2. Change-of-mind return accurately identifies Owen's stated reason, rather than inventing a fault or misdescription.
  2. return window Turn 4. Return window is the retailer's stated thirty-day period for this purchase, not a universal legal limit.
  3. goodwill exception Turn 6. Goodwill exception is a permitted discretionary departure from policy; requesting it does not establish approval or replace applicable rights.
  4. manager review Turn 8. Manager review is the assessment step Elena can submit; its result is not supplied or guaranteed.
  5. store credit Turn 10. Store credit is value for a future purchase; it also requires an actual available or approved basis here.
  6. proof of purchase Turn 12. Proof of purchase identifies the transaction and its details, supporting the review without requiring a fabricated outcome.
  7. exception authority Turn 14. Exception authority identifies who may approve a policy departure; Elena's submission does not transfer that decision power to her.
  8. fault allegation Turn 16. Fault allegation is a reported defect requiring its own assessment; it must not be automatically rejected under the goodwill window.
  9. complaint record Turn 18. Complaint record preserves the customer's actual concern, supplied details, and handling without turning a request into an approved remedy.
  10. service handoff Turn 20. Service handoff transfers accurate context and responsibility, including the distinction between a submitted review and its eventual outcome.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Owen: I paid eighty dollars for this jacket and have not worn it. I chose the wrong style. I am five days outside the return window; is a review possible?
  2. Elena: You are asking for a change-of-mind return on day thirty-five. I understand you would prefer not to keep an unworn item. Let me explain what I can do without promising a decision I cannot make.
  3. Owen: The receipt says thirty days. Is there any way to ask someone to look at it?
  4. Elena: Yes. The stated return window is thirty days for this purchase. We also need to check the actual transaction and applicable rights rather than treat that policy as a universal legal answer.
  5. Owen: I am not saying there is anything wrong with it. I simply chose the wrong style for myself.
  6. Elena: I will record that accurately. I can request a goodwill exception, but I cannot approve one myself. The store manager can assess the request under the relevant process.
  7. Owen: If you send it to the manager, does that mean I will get the money back?
  8. Elena: No. A manager review is a request for a decision, not the decision itself. I do not want you to hear a refund promise when I am offering a review.
  9. Owen: Would store credit be easier? I would be willing to buy something else here.
  10. Elena: I can include that preference, but store credit has not been approved either. I should not present it as an automatic substitute simply because the original request is outside the stated window.
  11. Owen: All right. What information does the manager need from me?
  12. Elena: We need the relevant proof of purchase, the item condition, and your requested remedy through the approved process. I will include what you have already told me so you do not have to start again.
  13. Owen: I have the receipt and the jacket with its tags. Please submit the request.
  14. Elena: I will. The manager has the exception authority described in our process. I will keep your reason and preferred outcomes clear rather than suggest the jacket is faulty to make the request sound stronger.
  15. Owen: Suppose I later notice a fault. Would the thirty-day window automatically rule that out too?
  16. Elena: A fault allegation would need to be recorded and assessed separately under the applicable rights and facts. I cannot decide that hypothetical claim from the change-of-mind policy alone.
  17. Owen: That is clear. For now, it is just the style, and I would like the review.
  18. Elena: The complaint record will show the eighty-dollar jacket, unworn condition, day thirty-five, and your preference. It will not say a refund or exchange has already been agreed.
  19. Owen: Please include the receipt details in the handoff. I would rather not start again when the manager responds.
  20. Elena: I will preserve that in the service handoff. The next response should communicate the actual assessment and explain the available next step, not make you reconstruct this conversation.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An outside-policy return needs review

Fictional retailer case: a business buyer requests a return outside the stated policy. The representative may explain approved options or request exception review, but cannot grant an exception.

  1. Business purchasing coordinator: Our return falls outside the stated policy. Can you approve it because we've bought from you before?
  2. Customer service supervisor: I can explain the approved options and request an exception review, but I can't grant one myself.
  3. Business purchasing coordinator: Then what choice can I take back to our accounts team without promising a refund?
  4. Customer service supervisor: We can review the applicable standard options or submit your request to the authorized decision maker.
  5. Business purchasing coordinator: If we choose the review, will our customer lifetime value guarantee a favorable answer?
  6. Customer service supervisor: No. I shouldn't predict the decision or invent different terms based on that measure.
  7. Business purchasing coordinator: Please submit the request and make clear that we haven't accepted any alternative yet.
  8. Customer service supervisor: I will. Any goodwill credit also needs the relevant authorization rather than an informal promise.
  9. Business purchasing coordinator: I'll tell the team the exception is requested, not approved, while we await a verified response.
  10. Customer service supervisor: That keeps the next step useful and the conditions explicit without changing the policy in this conversation.
A partial replacement is available under the case offer

Separate fictional case: an authorized service offer permits replacing one missing item now or waiting for a complete two-item bundle; bundle availability is unconfirmed and the buyer has not chosen.

  1. Customer account representative: Our approved offer is one replacement item now, or a complete bundle when its availability is confirmed.
  2. Business buyer: I need both items together, but could use the missing one sooner if necessary.
  3. Customer account representative: Then the tradeoff is earlier partial replacement versus waiting for the complete bundle's confirmed availability.
  4. Business buyer: Can you promise the bundle arrives next week if I choose to wait today?
  5. Customer account representative: No bundle timing is confirmed. I can request an update, but can't guarantee next week's arrival.
  6. Business buyer: Could I take the one item now and still receive the whole bundle later at no charge?
  7. Customer account representative: That combination isn't authorized by the supplied offer. I can ask for review, not approve it.
  8. Business buyer: I'll choose the single replacement under the stated offer rather than assume both benefits apply.
  9. Customer account representative: I'll record that choice and confirm the actual processing details through our approved service process.
  10. Business buyer: Please keep any separate goodwill-credit request distinct so the order record doesn't imply it's already granted.
A review call or an asynchronous status response

Separate fictional escalation case: a buyer wants an immediate senior review, but the service team can offer a booked review call or a status response after case review; timing must be agreed.

  1. Corporate buyer: I want a senior person to decide this return immediately while I'm on the phone.
  2. Escalation coordinator: I can request a review call or arrange a status response after review, but not promise an immediate decision.
  3. Corporate buyer: Which option lets me explain the circumstances directly instead of relying only on the existing notes?
  4. Escalation coordinator: The review call allows that discussion, provided we can agree an available appointment with the reviewer.
  5. Corporate buyer: Then book the call. Does choosing it guarantee an exception to the return policy?
  6. Escalation coordinator: No. Access to review doesn't determine the outcome or authorize a goodwill credit in advance.
  7. Corporate buyer: I'll take the call option, but I need you to confirm availability rather than leave it vague.
  8. Escalation coordinator: I'll request an achievable appointment and tell you whether the proposed timing has been accepted.
  9. Corporate buyer: Please don't advise me to start a chargeback as a way of forcing the reviewer to agree.
  10. Escalation coordinator: I won't. I'll keep this conversation focused on the retailer's approved review options and their conditions.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A return request beyond the goodwill window
  1. Read turns 1-10; acknowledge the request without promising an exception.
  2. Swap roles for turns 11-20; keep a change-of-mind request distinct from a fault report.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep another review within its authority

A customer requests a change-of-mind return on day 22 under a stated 14-day policy. Agent Noor may submit a review to manager Beth but cannot approve it. No outcome is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Noor: "The request is on day ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 22

Twenty-two is the request day stated for this separate transaction.

2. Customer: "The stated policy window is ___ days."

Answer and explanation

B. 14

Fourteen days is the fictional policy period, not an invented universal legal rule.

3. Noor: "The manager reviewing the request is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. Beth

Beth is the named manager; Noor only submits the review.

4. Customer: "Submission does not guarantee ___."

Answer and explanation

A. approval

A submitted review is not the same as an approved exception or remedy.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Customer Service and Escalations
Original fictional case: Customer Owen wants to return an unworn $80 jacket because he changed his mind. His request is on day 35; the retailer states a 30-day change-of-mind return window for this purchase. No fault or misdescription is alleged. Agent Elena cannot approve a goodwill exception but can submit a review to the store manager. No review outcome is supplied. Before treating the policy as decisive, the team must check any applicable customer rights and transaction facts. A fault complaint would require a different assessment, not an automatic rejection under the goodwill window.
Language workshop: A return request beyond the goodwill window
Communication goal: Acknowledge a customer request, explain a bounded exception review, and distinguish policy from applicable rights.
Language guidance: This is a change-of-mind request on day 35, not an established fault claim. Record the actual reason accurately. The 30-day policy is a fictional retailer rule for this purchase, not a universal statement of consumer law.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You would like to return the unworn jacket because you changed your mind. / This request is on day thirty-five. / The stated change-of-mind window for this purchase is thirty days. / We also need to check the rights applicable to the actual transaction. / I can submit the request, but I cannot approve an exception myself. / I can refer this to the store manager for review. / Submission does not guarantee a refund or exchange. / Please provide the purchase details through our approved process. / Would you like me to submit that review? / I will record this as a change-of-mind request. / If you are reporting a fault, I need to record and assess that separately. / Store credit has not been approved either. / I will include the details you have already supplied. / The manager holds the stated exception authority. / The record will show the timing, reason, item condition, and requested remedy. / I will communicate the actual review outcome without promising it in advance.
Vocabulary: change-of-mind return: A return requested because the customer no longer wants the item. / return window: The period specified for a return under particular terms. / goodwill exception: A discretionary departure from policy where permitted. / exception authority: The defined power to approve a policy departure. / proof of purchase: Evidence identifying a purchase and its relevant details. / purchase channel: The route through which the customer bought the item. / item condition: The physical state of a product. / original payment method: The payment route used for the original purchase. / store credit: Value available for a future purchase from the retailer. / exchange: Replacing a purchased item with another under relevant terms. / refund: Return of an amount paid. / manager review: Assessment by the person assigned the relevant decision authority. / fault allegation: A customer's statement that a product has a defect. / misdescription: A mismatch between an item and its stated description. / applicable rights: Customer protections relevant to the actual transaction. / statutory remedy: A remedy arising under the relevant law. / policy discretion: Permitted choice within an organization's policy authority. / escalation route: The defined path to a higher or specialist review. / complaint record: A documented account of a customer's concern and its handling. / case owner: The person responsible for coordinating the case. / review outcome: The result of an assessment. / reason code: A structured label describing the basis for a transaction or request. / resolution option: A possible way to address the customer's concern. / service handoff: Transfer of relevant context and responsibility to another team member.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Store Operations and Omnichannel ExecutionReady online, incomplete on the pickup shelf+

Your goal

Give an actionable shift handoff that corrects a false pickup status without hiding the missing item.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Ready online, incomplete on the pickup shelf

At 16:00, pickup order P64 shows ready online. It contains three items: a notebook, a pen set, and a mug. Staff have located and staged the notebook and pen set, but cannot find the mug. No substitute has been approved. Outgoing lead Talia must hand the issue to incoming lead Evan, who will contact the customer and update the case at 16:30. The local process requires every ordered item to be verified and staged before the whole order is marked ready. Partial pickup needs the permitted process and the customer's agreement.

Talia: Outgoing shift lead / Evan: Incoming shift lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which item has not been located?

Answer and explanation

A. The mug

The briefing identifies the mug as missing while the other two items are staged.

2. What does the local whole-order ready status require?

Answer and explanation

B. Every ordered item verified and staged

The local rule requires all ordered items, not just part of the order, to be verified and staged.

3. Who owns the next customer contact?

Answer and explanation

B. Evan

Evan is the incoming lead assigned to contact the customer and update the case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Two staged items do not make this three-item order ready under the stated local process. A missing mug is not evidence that it was collected, canceled, or refunded. Keep each status tied to its actual event.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
omnichannel
Coordinated retail activity across store and digital channels.coordinate omnichannel execution
buy online, pick up in store (BOPIS)
Ordering online for collection at a store.fulfill a BOPIS order
click and collect
An online order collected from a designated location.manage click-and-collect orders
order management system (OMS)
Software coordinating order information and fulfillment status.update the OMS
point of sale (POS)
The system or place where a retail sale is processed.reconcile POS records
picking
Retrieving the specified items for an order.complete order picking
pick list
A list of items and quantities to retrieve.verify the pick list
staging
Placing picked items in the designated holding area.confirm order staging
pickup shelf
The area holding orders prepared for collection.check the pickup shelf
ready-for-pickup status
A status indicating the specified readiness conditions are met.correct ready-for-pickup status
short pick
A pick in which the required quantity cannot be found or supplied.record a short pick
item-level status
The state recorded for an individual order item.update item-level status
order-level status
The overall state recorded for the complete order.correct order-level status
substitution
Supplying a different item in place of the ordered one.confirm a substitution
customer consent
The customer's agreement to a proposed action.obtain customer consent
partial pickup
Collection of only part of the ordered items.arrange partial pickup
pickup notification
A message telling the customer about collection readiness.correct a pickup notification
collection verification
Checking the required identity or order details at pickup.complete collection verification
cancellation line
An order item recorded for cancellation under the process.confirm a cancellation line
refund status
The actual stage of returning the relevant payment.verify refund status
shift handoff
Transfer of work status and responsibility between shifts.give a shift handoff
action owner
The person responsible for a specified next action.name the action owner
discrepancy log
A record of mismatches requiring follow-up.update the discrepancy log
resolution checkpoint
A scheduled point to review progress on an open issue.set a resolution checkpoint

Partial pickup and substitution are different options. Neither should be assumed from silence or staff convenience. Use the permitted process, obtain the necessary customer agreement, and preserve the remaining item and payment status.

C Notice the language

Handover language must support the next action

A cheerful all sorted can conceal an unfinished customer problem. Name the item, the physical status, the system discrepancy, and the next owner. The incoming colleague needs enough detail to act without repeating the search or misleading the customer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Start with the order
This handoff concerns pickup order P64.
State the verified items
The notebook and pen set are located and staged.
Name the gap
The mug has not been found.
Identify the contradiction
The website says ready, but the order is incomplete.
Apply the local rule
Whole-order readiness requires all three items to be verified and staged.
Correct the visible status
We need to correct the ready message through the approved process.
Protect the remaining items
Keep the two staged items identified with this order.
Reject an assumed substitute
No substitute has been approved.
Offer a bounded choice
Check whether partial pickup is permitted and whether the customer agrees.
Assign the next action
Evan will contact the customer.
Set the checkpoint
Update the case at 16:30.
Limit the promise
That is a review time, not a promise that the mug will be found.
Keep money status separate
Do not mark a refund complete unless it has actually reached that status.
Reduce repeated work
Record which locations have already been checked.
Preserve the open item
The mug remains unresolved until its actual disposition is recorded.
Close the handoff
Confirm the owner, customer message, and outstanding item before leaving.
6 language and precision notes
Located and staged
Describes physical preparation, not simply a system entry.
Has not been found
States the search result without inventing theft or cancellation.
Through the approved process
Makes the correction traceable and properly authorized.
Whether ... and whether ...
Separates process permission from customer agreement.
Not a promise
Keeps a scheduled checkpoint from becoming a guaranteed result.
Actual disposition
Refers to what truly happened to the missing order line.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff is actionable?

Answer and explanation

D. P64: two items staged, mug missing, ready status incorrect; Evan contacts customer and updates at 16:30.

The detailed handoff preserves physical facts, the system mismatch, responsibility, and the next checkpoint.

2. Which substitution claim is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. No substitute has been approved.

The briefing explicitly leaves substitution unapproved and does not supply customer consent.

3. What does 16:30 mean?

Answer and explanation

B. A case-update checkpoint

Sixteen thirty is the scheduled update, not proof of any particular resolution outcome.

4. Which record would invent an event?

Answer and explanation

A. Marking the missing mug as collected because staff cannot find it

Failure to locate an item is not evidence that the customer collected it.
